Here's a summary of what the Austin City Council did at its July 23, 2026 meeting:

Thursday, July 23, 2026 Regular Meeting

Here's a summary of what the Austin City Council did at its July 23, 2026 meeting:

  • Major "Dog's Head" Development Advanced:

    Council moved forward with plans for the "Dog's Head" area, a large 2,645-acre tract. This included approving an agreement with Travis County, a preliminary financing plan, and ordinances to vacate public roadways to help establish Tax Increment Reinvestment Zone (TIRZ) No. 21 for the area's development.

  • Affordable Housing and Increased Housing Options:

    Six acres on Bolm Road were sold to the Austin Housing Finance Corporation for a future affordable housing development, a move highlighted by Council Member Velazquez as a positive repurposing of the land. Additionally, Council approved changes to city rules to allow more multifamily housing (up to four units) in certain areas, aiming to boost housing density.

  • New Rules for Large Water Users:

    Concerned about Austin's water supply, Council approved a resolution directing the City Manager to closely track and evaluate how much water large businesses, particularly data centers, use. This initiative aims to ensure water conservation and fair usage, though some advocates felt stronger mandates, like requiring closed-loop cooling, were "watered down."

  • Big Investments in Energy Future:

    A substantial 20-year contract, worth up to $320 million, was approved for a utility-scale battery storage facility to enhance Austin's electrical grid. Separately, Council adopted a resolution to explore and expand geothermal energy, seeking to create cleaner energy sources and new job opportunities.

  • Street Renamings to Reflect Austin's Values:

    In an effort to address historical names tied to the Confederacy, two streets were renamed. Paisano Trail became Zion Rest Lane, and Plantation Road was officially renamed Tommie Lee Wyatt Lane, honoring a prominent local civil rights leader. The location of the Plantation Road renaming sparked some public debate.

  • Key Initiatives Postponed or Withdrawn:

    A resolution to call a November 2026 bond election was pulled from the agenda. An ordinance to establish a process for managing condemnations of city-owned parkland was also withdrawn. Separately, a public hearing regarding an appeal for a conditional use permit for the Girls' School of Austin was postponed until September 10, 2026, and two zoning requests for the "Saxon Acres 2" area were indefinitely postponed.

There's also reclaimed water requirements through our purple pipe system, and it creates data reporting and transparency rules as well. I've had a lot of heard a lot of people today talk about what that plan means, and I'm here, hopefully to shed a little light on that. Many of those issues still exist today. The fascinating paradox of these concerns is that the mining companies hadn't encroached, had not encroached on most of these communities. These communities had encroached on the mining. Our region's rapid expansion drove people to the doorstep of our industrial mineral basket in search of affordable housing or rural lifestyle. And, of course, [5:44:04 PM] the natural beauty of the Colorado river itself. When we completed the Colorado river corridor plan in 2012, a staggering 36% of that 3000 acre corridor, or 11000 acres, was owned by mining interests. I'm not sure what the number is today. Historical mines in that Austin area gave us the great lawn at zilker park, the large lagoon at the end of lady bird lake, across from the Oracle campus. And that brings us to dogs head, a four square mile area that has served as a literal mineral basket of our city since the 1930s. For nearly a century, this land gave its weight so that Austin could grow. When this council unanimously approved the corridor plan in June of 2012, the executive summary explicitly anticipated the moment we're at today, the plan states, and I quote, once the mining operations are completed, the land may be repurposed for new community uses. Large areas of the corridor can thus transition from mining uses to [5:45:06 PM] future urbanized areas made of schools, neighborhoods, businesses, and services, all integrated into a network of parks, green belts and farmland. I began studying the dogs head in 2017, and today I'm working to help create the vision plan. If you walk that land right now, you will see the scars of the past are still present. We're. Legacy from old dragline strip mining, a severely fractured ecological system, degraded roadways, and an entirely absent riparian drainage corridor within the property itself, to name only a few of the issues that exist. This land is not pristine. It is not a static moment in time to be protected. It is a degraded industrial site that desperately needs to be healed. We have a rare opportunity now to fulfill the promise of the corridor plan. We can turn these industrial scars into a [5:46:06 PM] vibrant, mixed use, mixed income community. We can create a new destination for Austin, one that balances industry and walkable neighborhoods connected by parks and trails, one that restores missing riparian corridors for wildlife, solves regional mobility issues, and brings grocery stores, health care, and schools to community that has long been left behind. But standard development tools cannot fix 90 years of heavy industrial mining. The sheer scale of the ecological degradation, the infrastructure deficit and the erosion control required on this site is immense. We cannot expect typical private development to absorb the cost of reclaiming a century's worth of mining impact. That is precisely why we need the tirz. For the dogs head, the tirz is the exact mechanism designed for this challenge. It allows the city to leverage the future value by creating this project to fund the massive foundational public infrastructure and [5:47:06 PM] environmental restoration required today, without affecting the general fund. By approving this tirz you're not just approving a project, you're choosing to heal a degraded landscape and turn Austin's historical mineral basket into a resilient public asset for the next century. Environmental racism is a term coined by doctor Benjamin Chavez Jr, the current president and CEO of the national newspaper publishers association and association of African-American newspaper publishers from across the country. Doctor Chavez has been a lifelong advocate for the black community and other marginalized groups. He has been called the grandfather of environmental justice movement to honor the work that doctor Chavez has done throughout his life. I want to educate everybody about what environmental racism is and what it is not. Environmental [7:13:47 PM] racism is the disproportionate exposure of marginalized communities to environmental hazards like toxic toxic waste sites, landfills, industrial pollution. It is discriminatory policies, unequal enforcement of regulations, and the intentional placement of hazardous facilities and marginalized neighborhoods. Environmental racism is not nimbyism dressed up as social justice. Using the term for this project is a dog whistle, and it cheapens the experience of true systemic environmental injustice. I'm going to assume that the community members misusing this term are so fortunate as to not have experienced systemic racism, and are unable to recognize it when it happens and understand what it is and what it is not.
